Letâs get started. How much is 10GB divided by 15M, exactly?Ten Great Britains divided by 15 million gives around half of kilo britain. Not much. If we run all our guests in our runtime, our custom scheduler needs to switch between them. Whatâs the absolute minimum state we need to preserve for each guest? We can ignore server initialisation and assume switches only happen within the main loop. Letâs take another look at the loop.for (int client; (client = accept(server, NULL, NULL)) >= 0;) { if (recv(client, NULL, 4096, MSG_TRUNC) > 0) send(client, reply, sizeof reply - 1, MSG_NOSIGNAL); close(client); }This code has only two state variables, server and client. Both are simple file descriptors. If our scheduler had to suspend a guest at any syscall point in this loop, weâd need to save the following state:struct Context { pc: u64, // Instruction address to return to fd_server: i32, // server file descriptor fd_client: i32, // client file descriptor }We only need 16 bytes per guest, so to run 15M guests in our runtime and be able to suspend them and switch between them, we would need 220MB of RAM. Weâre playing it safe here - we donât need the full u64 or i32, and with bit packing, we could probably get down to 8 bytes per guest. What else do we need?$ codex "How much TCP connection takes?" Idle connection takes zero, while active connection consumes around 5kbHereâs what we get if we assume that only 1M of connections are active at any given moment.14M idle connections - 0 bytesScheduling state for 15M guests - 220MB1M active connections at 5kb each = 5GBOnly half of our RAM budget, yay! I donât often exceed my own expectations. Letâs validate it:int main(void) { // Run 14M of idle connections - just listen and do nothing // SHOULD CONSUMER ZERO MEMORY! for (int i = 0; i < 14_000_000; i++) { int server = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0); listen(server, 1) } }$ ./run_15m_idle_servers Out Of Memory $ dmesg | tail -n 1 'SHOULD CONSUMER ZERO MEMORY' - what are you, stupid?Looks like the kernel still has some leftover comments from Linus. Iâm not stupid, but I have to admit I was below average here. While an LLM is a significant boost for learning, itâs too easy to accept its answers without thinking critically and feel ashamed later on. But hey, embarrassment is a cost of entry - letâs dive into networking in the kernel.Networking in the kernelRemember when I said that our simple server uses only 5 syscalls? I missed one that was right in front of me. Let me highlight it:int main(void) { for (int i = 0; i < 10_000_000; ++i) { socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0); } }That code will OOM in a 10GB VM. The socket syscall allocates around 3300 bytes, whether the socket is active, listening, or doing nothing. Linux was clearly written at a time when RAM was big and cheap, sigh. Letâs ask AI where all the memory goes:StructsizeofDescriptionstruct tcp_sock2240BTCP state, queues, timers, sequence numbers, congestion control, retransmit and delivery bookkeepingstruct socket_alloc768BA 128-byte struct socket with an embedded wait queue, plus a 608-byte sockfs inode and paddingstruct file184BOpen-file operations, flags, credentials, reference count, and the pointer back to the socketstruct dentry192BThe sockfs pseudo-name, inode pointer, and VFS lookup and lifetime bookkeepingTotal3384BPotato-potato, TLDR, the kernel uses that memory for many things. I was hoping to see some buffer: [u8; 3300], but it doesnât look like we can optimise much without rewriting half the kernel. Itâs Sunday and I have few tokens left, so weâll have to think instead - how can we bypass the kernel?What are our options? Engineering wisdom tells us that if we canât solve a task, weâre working at the wrong level of abstraction. Hmâ¦ level, level, layer. Wait a minute! We started with HTTP and went level down to TCP/IP. That reminds me of something! Mom! Mom! Where are all my university books? I NEED THEM NOW!The OSI model isâ¦ umâ¦ something I studied at university. Yeah, thatâs a good definition. We started with our app and HTTP at the application layer, then went down to TCP/IP at the transport layer. There are three more layers below that. Can we go lower to get full control and solve our RAM problem? Oh yes, thatâs exactly what operating at the network and data link layers lets us do. What about going to the lowest?Clear.Each layer wraps the data from the layer above it.HTTPApplication Â· L7TCPTransport Â· L4IPNetwork Â· L3EthernetData link Â· L2PhysicalL1GET /PayloadPayloadPayloadâ¡ð©Src port50123Dst port80Seq1001ACK7001FlagsACKSrc IP192.0.2.10Dst IP198.51.100.20TTL64Protocol6 (TCP)Dst MAC02:00:00:00:00:feSrc MAC02:00:00:00:00:01EtherType0x0800 (IPv4)FCSCRCTCP/IP is stateful, but everything below is stateless. None of the lower layers know or care about the payload, so nothing prevents us from sending new IP packets or even Ethernet frames with our own handcrafted binary data. We just need to reimplement the TCP and IP stack ourselves. Are there any examples? Embedded folks have solved this millions of times. The most extreme case is Adam Dunkelsâs Miniweb - it combines a custom TCP/IP implementation and an HTTP server that together need only 28 bytes (B-Y-T-E-S) to work.There are plenty of less extreme, more mature implementations, though:gVisor essentially reimplements the whole OS, including the network stackSeastar is a whole runtime with its own network stack from the amazing team behind ScyllaDBlwIP is a popular C implementation of TCP/IPF-Stack uses FreeBSDâs TCP/IP stackGo, C++, and C are nice languages (well, kinda), but theyâre nowhere near as nice as our beloved Rust. So weâll take smoltcp, which is purely amazing:>smoltcp is a standalone, event-driven TCP/IP stack that is designed for >bare-metal, real-time systems. smoltcp does not need heap allocation at allSo, cargo add smoltcp and call it a day? Not so fast. smoltcp is essentially a smart parser and canât do I/O. How does it communicate with your network card? There are 3 ways:TUN is a virtual network interface that the kernel provides. It operates at the IP layerTAP is another kernel feature that operates one layer down, at the Ethernet layerDPDK lets you communicate directly with the network card using Ethernet frames, completely bypassing the kernelsmoltcp is kind enough to provide the first two options out of the box, and theyâre easy to use.fn main() -> std::io::Result<()> { let (mut device, config) = match std::env::args().nth(1).as_deref() { Some("tun") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltun", Medium::Ip)?, Config::new(HardwareAddress::Ip), ), Some("tap") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltap", Medium::Ethernet)?, Config::new(EthernetAddress([0x02, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2]).into()), ), _ => panic!("pass tun or tap"), }; let mut iface = Interface::new(config, &mut device, Instant::now()); iface.update_ip_addrs(|addrs| { addrs.push(IpCidr::new(IpAddress::v4(192, 0, 2, 2), 24)).unwrap(); }); let mut sockets = SocketSet::new(Vec::new()); // Main network loop loop { let now = Instant::now(); iface.poll(now, &mut device, &mut sockets); wait(device.as_raw_fd(), iface.poll_delay(now, &sockets))?; } }But what about the third option, DPDK? When you care about latency A LOT, every syscall is a bloody wound. The syscall itself might take 500ns, and copying data from the kernel to userspace might take another 1000ns, depending on the buffer size. That sounds tiny, but for perspective: itâs common for high-frequency trading firms to saturate 400Gbs network interfaces. That means processing up to 600 million packets per second, or roughly 1.7ns per packet.DPDK lets your program communicate directly with the hardware network card using Ethernet frames, bypassing the entire kernel network stack. You read from and write to a memory-mapped buffer without syscalls: when you want to send data - you simply write it to the buffer and youâre done; when you want to read what youâve received - itâs already there.Thereâs another, less obvious advantage - what happens when a new packet arrives at your network device? An interrupt fires, the CPU core stops executing your app, switches to the kernel, processes the interrupt, and then goes back to your app. Now imagine A LOT of incoming packets, so many that your app gets interrupted all the time. It has a beautiful name, livelock, and I guess debugging it in production is its own level of hell. DPDK lets you avoid all that, but thereâs a cost.DPDK works by running a busy loop that takes up an entire CPU core. It constantly moves data between memory and the network device in both directions, kinda like that:loop { send_from_app_memory_to_network(); receive_from_network_to_app_memory(); }Thereâs another, less obvious nuance. DPDK requires exclusive access to the network device because it uses a driver that operates at the hardware level - two drivers canât work with the same device at the same time. Once you enable the DPDK driver for your network interfaceâ¦ it disappears from the OS. Yep, nothing in ifconfig, if you enable it over SSH, youâll lose SSH access too. I can imagine a lot of funny stories coming out of that.Actually, thereâs a fourth option now - Linux now has an AF_XDP subsystem that does something similar. It lets you bypass the kernel network stack and uses the same idea of memory-mapped buffers to get zero-copy I/O in your app. The kernel still owns the device and interrupts still happen as usual. When a new packet arrives, the kernel runs a simple eBPF filter that decides whether to send it directly to the userspace buffer, bypassing the network stack, or process it normally. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought. And another article.Referenceshttps://github.com/artemyarulin/AI10M/tree/main/poc/ten-million-webservers sourcehttps://medium.com/beyond-localhost/how-we-handle-15M-websocket-connections-on-a-single-server-8917c3952f59 great overview of DPDKhttps://doc.dpdk.org/guides/index.html DPDK main documentation sitehttps://docs.kernel.org/networking/af_xdp.html AF_XDP overview

Letâs use a beautiful technology called AnyIP, which lets us listen on a whole subnet. Many VPS and dedicated server providers routinely give you a whole TCPv6 subnet. We can simply run sudo ip -6 route add local 2001:db8:1234:5678::/64 dev lo to get 18 quintillion addresses (plus or minus one) to listen on. You can do the same for IPv4 - acquiring a subnet with 15M addresses would cost around $250 million, so adjust the command for your environment. Letâs ask AI where all the memory goes:StructsizeofDescriptionstruct tcp_sock2240BTCP state, queues, timers, sequence numbers, congestion control, retransmit and delivery bookkeepingstruct socket_alloc768BA 128-byte struct socket with an embedded wait queue, plus a 608-byte sockfs inode and paddingstruct file184BOpen-file operations, flags, credentials, reference count, and the pointer back to the socketstruct dentry192BThe sockfs pseudo-name, inode pointer, and VFS lookup and lifetime bookkeepingTotal3384BPotato-potato, TLDR, the kernel uses that memory for many things. I was hoping to see some buffer: [u8; 3300], but it doesnât look like we can optimise much without rewriting half the kernel. Itâs Sunday and I have few tokens left, so weâll have to think instead - how can we bypass the kernel?What are our options? Engineering wisdom tells us that if we canât solve a task, weâre working at the wrong level of abstraction. Hmâ¦ level, level, layer. Wait a minute! We started with HTTP and went level down to TCP/IP. That reminds me of something! Mom! Mom! Where are all my university books? I NEED THEM NOW!The OSI model isâ¦ umâ¦ something I studied at university. Yeah, thatâs a good definition. We started with our app and HTTP at the application layer, then went down to TCP/IP at the transport layer. There are three more layers below that. Can we go lower to get full control and solve our RAM problem? Oh yes, thatâs exactly what operating at the network and data link layers lets us do. What about going to the lowest?Clear.Each layer wraps the data from the layer above it.HTTPApplication Â· L7TCPTransport Â· L4IPNetwork Â· L3EthernetData link Â· L2PhysicalL1GET /PayloadPayloadPayloadâ¡ð©Src port50123Dst port80Seq1001ACK7001FlagsACKSrc IP192.0.2.10Dst IP198.51.100.20TTL64Protocol6 (TCP)Dst MAC02:00:00:00:00:feSrc MAC02:00:00:00:00:01EtherType0x0800 (IPv4)FCSCRCTCP/IP is stateful, but everything below is stateless. None of the lower layers know or care about the payload, so nothing prevents us from sending new IP packets or even Ethernet frames with our own handcrafted binary data. We just need to reimplement the TCP and IP stack ourselves. Are there any examples? Embedded folks have solved this millions of times. The most extreme case is Adam Dunkelsâs Miniweb - it combines a custom TCP/IP implementation and an HTTP server that together need only 28 bytes (B-Y-T-E-S) to work.There are plenty of less extreme, more mature implementations, though:gVisor essentially reimplements the whole OS, including the network stackSeastar is a whole runtime with its own network stack from the amazing team behind ScyllaDBlwIP is a popular C implementation of TCP/IPF-Stack uses FreeBSDâs TCP/IP stackGo, C++, and C are nice languages (well, kinda), but theyâre nowhere near as nice as our beloved Rust. So weâll take smoltcp, which is purely amazing:>smoltcp is a standalone, event-driven TCP/IP stack that is designed for >bare-metal, real-time systems. smoltcp does not need heap allocation at allSo, cargo add smoltcp and call it a day? Not so fast. smoltcp is essentially a smart parser and canât do I/O. How does it communicate with your network card? There are 3 ways:TUN is a virtual network interface that the kernel provides. It operates at the IP layerTAP is another kernel feature that operates one layer down, at the Ethernet layerDPDK lets you communicate directly with the network card using Ethernet frames, completely bypassing the kernelsmoltcp is kind enough to provide the first two options out of the box, and theyâre easy to use.fn main() -> std::io::Result<()> { let (mut device, config) = match std::env::args().nth(1).as_deref() { Some("tun") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltun", Medium::Ip)?, Config::new(HardwareAddress::Ip), ), Some("tap") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltap", Medium::Ethernet)?, Config::new(EthernetAddress([0x02, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2]).into()), ), _ => panic!("pass tun or tap"), }; let mut iface = Interface::new(config, &mut device, Instant::now()); iface.update_ip_addrs(|addrs| { addrs.push(IpCidr::new(IpAddress::v4(192, 0, 2, 2), 24)).unwrap(); }); let mut sockets = SocketSet::new(Vec::new()); // Main network loop loop { let now = Instant::now(); iface.poll(now, &mut device, &mut sockets); wait(device.as_raw_fd(), iface.poll_delay(now, &sockets))?; } }But what about the third option, DPDK? When you care about latency A LOT, every syscall is a bloody wound. The syscall itself might take 500ns, and copying data from the kernel to userspace might take another 1000ns, depending on the buffer size. That sounds tiny, but for perspective: itâs common for high-frequency trading firms to saturate 400Gbs network interfaces. That means processing up to 600 million packets per second, or roughly 1.7ns per packet.DPDK lets your program communicate directly with the hardware network card using Ethernet frames, bypassing the entire kernel network stack. You read from and write to a memory-mapped buffer without syscalls: when you want to send data - you simply write it to the buffer and youâre done; when you want to read what youâve received - itâs already there.Thereâs another, less obvious advantage - what happens when a new packet arrives at your network device? An interrupt fires, the CPU core stops executing your app, switches to the kernel, processes the interrupt, and then goes back to your app. Now imagine A LOT of incoming packets, so many that your app gets interrupted all the time. It has a beautiful name, livelock, and I guess debugging it in production is its own level of hell. DPDK lets you avoid all that, but thereâs a cost.DPDK works by running a busy loop that takes up an entire CPU core. It constantly moves data between memory and the network device in both directions, kinda like that:loop { send_from_app_memory_to_network(); receive_from_network_to_app_memory(); }Thereâs another, less obvious nuance. DPDK requires exclusive access to the network device because it uses a driver that operates at the hardware level - two drivers canât work with the same device at the same time. Once you enable the DPDK driver for your network interfaceâ¦ it disappears from the OS. Yep, nothing in ifconfig, if you enable it over SSH, youâll lose SSH access too. I can imagine a lot of funny stories coming out of that.Actually, thereâs a fourth option now - Linux now has an AF_XDP subsystem that does something similar. It lets you bypass the kernel network stack and uses the same idea of memory-mapped buffers to get zero-copy I/O in your app. The kernel still owns the device and interrupts still happen as usual. When a new packet arrives, the kernel runs a simple eBPF filter that decides whether to send it directly to the userspace buffer, bypassing the network stack, or process it normally. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought. And another article.Referenceshttps://github.com/artemyarulin/AI10M/tree/main/poc/ten-million-webservers sourcehttps://medium.com/beyond-localhost/how-we-handle-15M-websocket-connections-on-a-single-server-8917c3952f59 great overview of DPDKhttps://doc.dpdk.org/guides/index.html DPDK main documentation sitehttps://docs.kernel.org/networking/af_xdp.html AF_XDP overview

Hm, one server consumes 1.5MB of RAM, so running that many would takeâ¦ 20TB of RAM, which is a few bytes more than our 10GB budget.RuntimeBy pure coincidence (what are the odds?), I explained in Running 10 million Codex CLIs on one server - LFI processes why running millions of processes creates too much overhead. The answer is to run them all in one process inside a custom runtime, much as V8 can run millions of guests in a single process. In Userland Linux scheduler in Rust, I showed how to build a specialised userland scheduler and switch between tasks with the absolute minimum amount of RAM per task.TLDR: Weâre using LFI for sandboxing. It gives us memory isolation and replaces all syscalls with calls to our host handler, which lets us implement I/O however we like. To schedule multiple guests, we load them all into our main processâs memory and make the CPU constantly switch between guests - we become our own micro OS.Since we need to save every byte and CPU cycle we can, weâre doomed to build another specialised runtime. Letâs get started. How much is 10GB divided by 15M, exactly?Ten Great Britains divided by 15 million gives around half of kilo britain. Not much. If we run all our guests in our runtime, our custom scheduler needs to switch between them. Whatâs the absolute minimum state we need to preserve for each guest? We can ignore server initialisation and assume switches only happen within the main loop. Letâs take another look at the loop.for (int client; (client = accept(server, NULL, NULL)) >= 0;) { if (recv(client, NULL, 4096, MSG_TRUNC) > 0) send(client, reply, sizeof reply - 1, MSG_NOSIGNAL); close(client); }This code has only two state variables, server and client. Both are simple file descriptors. If our scheduler had to suspend a guest at any syscall point in this loop, weâd need to save the following state:struct Context { pc: u64, // Instruction address to return to fd_server: i32, // server file descriptor fd_client: i32, // client file descriptor }We only need 16 bytes per guest, so to run 15M guests in our runtime and be able to suspend them and switch between them, we would need 220MB of RAM. Weâre playing it safe here - we donât need the full u64 or i32, and with bit packing, we could probably get down to 8 bytes per guest. What else do we need?$ codex "How much TCP connection takes?" Idle connection takes zero, while active connection consumes around 5kbHereâs what we get if we assume that only 1M of connections are active at any given moment.14M idle connections - 0 bytesScheduling state for 15M guests - 220MB1M active connections at 5kb each = 5GBOnly half of our RAM budget, yay! I donât often exceed my own expectations. Letâs validate it:int main(void) { // Run 14M of idle connections - just listen and do nothing // SHOULD CONSUMER ZERO MEMORY! for (int i = 0; i < 14_000_000; i++) { int server = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0); listen(server, 1) } }$ ./run_15m_idle_servers Out Of Memory $ dmesg | tail -n 1 'SHOULD CONSUMER ZERO MEMORY' - what are you, stupid?Looks like the kernel still has some leftover comments from Linus. It operates at the IP layerTAP is another kernel feature that operates one layer down, at the Ethernet layerDPDK lets you communicate directly with the network card using Ethernet frames, completely bypassing the kernelsmoltcp is kind enough to provide the first two options out of the box, and theyâre easy to use.fn main() -> std::io::Result<()> { let (mut device, config) = match std::env::args().nth(1).as_deref() { Some("tun") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltun", Medium::Ip)?, Config::new(HardwareAddress::Ip), ), Some("tap") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltap", Medium::Ethernet)?, Config::new(EthernetAddress([0x02, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2]).into()), ), _ => panic!("pass tun or tap"), }; let mut iface = Interface::new(config, &mut device, Instant::now()); iface.update_ip_addrs(|addrs| { addrs.push(IpCidr::new(IpAddress::v4(192, 0, 2, 2), 24)).unwrap(); }); let mut sockets = SocketSet::new(Vec::new()); // Main network loop loop { let now = Instant::now(); iface.poll(now, &mut device, &mut sockets); wait(device.as_raw_fd(), iface.poll_delay(now, &sockets))?; } }But what about the third option, DPDK? When you care about latency A LOT, every syscall is a bloody wound. The syscall itself might take 500ns, and copying data from the kernel to userspace might take another 1000ns, depending on the buffer size. That sounds tiny, but for perspective: itâs common for high-frequency trading firms to saturate 400Gbs network interfaces. That means processing up to 600 million packets per second, or roughly 1.7ns per packet.DPDK lets your program communicate directly with the hardware network card using Ethernet frames, bypassing the entire kernel network stack. You read from and write to a memory-mapped buffer without syscalls: when you want to send data - you simply write it to the buffer and youâre done; when you want to read what youâve received - itâs already there.Thereâs another, less obvious advantage - what happens when a new packet arrives at your network device? An interrupt fires, the CPU core stops executing your app, switches to the kernel, processes the interrupt, and then goes back to your app. Now imagine A LOT of incoming packets, so many that your app gets interrupted all the time. It has a beautiful name, livelock, and I guess debugging it in production is its own level of hell. DPDK lets you avoid all that, but thereâs a cost.DPDK works by running a busy loop that takes up an entire CPU core. It constantly moves data between memory and the network device in both directions, kinda like that:loop { send_from_app_memory_to_network(); receive_from_network_to_app_memory(); }Thereâs another, less obvious nuance. DPDK requires exclusive access to the network device because it uses a driver that operates at the hardware level - two drivers canât work with the same device at the same time. Once you enable the DPDK driver for your network interfaceâ¦ it disappears from the OS. Yep, nothing in ifconfig, if you enable it over SSH, youâll lose SSH access too. I can imagine a lot of funny stories coming out of that.Actually, thereâs a fourth option now - Linux now has an AF_XDP subsystem that does something similar. It lets you bypass the kernel network stack and uses the same idea of memory-mapped buffers to get zero-copy I/O in your app. The kernel still owns the device and interrupts still happen as usual. When a new packet arrives, the kernel runs a simple eBPF filter that decides whether to send it directly to the userspace buffer, bypassing the network stack, or process it normally. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought. And another article.Referenceshttps://github.com/artemyarulin/AI10M/tree/main/poc/ten-million-webservers sourcehttps://medium.com/beyond-localhost/how-we-handle-15M-websocket-connections-on-a-single-server-8917c3952f59 great overview of DPDKhttps://doc.dpdk.org/guides/index.html DPDK main documentation sitehttps://docs.kernel.org/networking/af_xdp.html AF_XDP overview

TLDR: Weâre using LFI for sandboxing. It gives us memory isolation and replaces all syscalls with calls to our host handler, which lets us implement I/O however we like. To schedule multiple guests, we load them all into our main processâs memory and make the CPU constantly switch between guests - we become our own micro OS.

Since we need to save every byte and CPU cycle we can, weâre doomed to build another specialised runtime. Letâs get started. How much is 10GB divided by 15M, exactly?Ten Great Britains divided by 15 million gives around half of kilo britain. Not much. If we run all our guests in our runtime, our custom scheduler needs to switch between them. Whatâs the absolute minimum state we need to preserve for each guest? We can ignore server initialisation and assume switches only happen within the main loop. Letâs take another look at the loop.for (int client; (client = accept(server, NULL, NULL)) >= 0;) { if (recv(client, NULL, 4096, MSG_TRUNC) > 0) send(client, reply, sizeof reply - 1, MSG_NOSIGNAL); close(client); }This code has only two state variables, server and client. Both are simple file descriptors. If our scheduler had to suspend a guest at any syscall point in this loop, weâd need to save the following state:struct Context { pc: u64, // Instruction address to return to fd_server: i32, // server file descriptor fd_client: i32, // client file descriptor }We only need 16 bytes per guest, so to run 15M guests in our runtime and be able to suspend them and switch between them, we would need 220MB of RAM. Weâre playing it safe here - we donât need the full u64 or i32, and with bit packing, we could probably get down to 8 bytes per guest. What else do we need?$ codex "How much TCP connection takes?" Idle connection takes zero, while active connection consumes around 5kbHereâs what we get if we assume that only 1M of connections are active at any given moment.14M idle connections - 0 bytesScheduling state for 15M guests - 220MB1M active connections at 5kb each = 5GBOnly half of our RAM budget, yay! I donât often exceed my own expectations. Letâs validate it:int main(void) { // Run 14M of idle connections - just listen and do nothing // SHOULD CONSUMER ZERO MEMORY! for (int i = 0; i < 14_000_000; i++) { int server = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0); listen(server, 1) } }$ ./run_15m_idle_servers Out Of Memory $ dmesg | tail -n 1 'SHOULD CONSUMER ZERO MEMORY' - what are you, stupid?Looks like the kernel still has some leftover comments from Linus. Iâm not stupid, but I have to admit I was below average here. While an LLM is a significant boost for learning, itâs too easy to accept its answers without thinking critically and feel ashamed later on. But hey, embarrassment is a cost of entry - letâs dive into networking in the kernel.Networking in the kernelRemember when I said that our simple server uses only 5 syscalls? I missed one that was right in front of me. Let me highlight it:int main(void) { for (int i = 0; i < 10_000_000; ++i) { socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0); } }That code will OOM in a 10GB VM. The socket syscall allocates around 3300 bytes, whether the socket is active, listening, or doing nothing. Linux was clearly written at a time when RAM was big and cheap, sigh. Letâs ask AI where all the memory goes:StructsizeofDescriptionstruct tcp_sock2240BTCP state, queues, timers, sequence numbers, congestion control, retransmit and delivery bookkeepingstruct socket_alloc768BA 128-byte struct socket with an embedded wait queue, plus a 608-byte sockfs inode and paddingstruct file184BOpen-file operations, flags, credentials, reference count, and the pointer back to the socketstruct dentry192BThe sockfs pseudo-name, inode pointer, and VFS lookup and lifetime bookkeepingTotal3384BPotato-potato, TLDR, the kernel uses that memory for many things. I was hoping to see some buffer: [u8; 3300], but it doesnât look like we can optimise much without rewriting half the kernel. Itâs Sunday and I have few tokens left, so weâll have to think instead - how can we bypass the kernel?What are our options? Engineering wisdom tells us that if we canât solve a task, weâre working at the wrong level of abstraction. Hmâ¦ level, level, layer. Wait a minute! We started with HTTP and went level down to TCP/IP. That reminds me of something! Mom! Mom! Where are all my university books? I NEED THEM NOW!The OSI model isâ¦ umâ¦ something I studied at university. Yeah, thatâs a good definition. We started with our app and HTTP at the application layer, then went down to TCP/IP at the transport layer. There are three more layers below that. Can we go lower to get full control and solve our RAM problem? Oh yes, thatâs exactly what operating at the network and data link layers lets us do. What about going to the lowest?Clear.Each layer wraps the data from the layer above it.HTTPApplication Â· L7TCPTransport Â· L4IPNetwork Â· L3EthernetData link Â· L2PhysicalL1GET /PayloadPayloadPayloadâ¡ð©Src port50123Dst port80Seq1001ACK7001FlagsACKSrc IP192.0.2.10Dst IP198.51.100.20TTL64Protocol6 (TCP)Dst MAC02:00:00:00:00:feSrc MAC02:00:00:00:00:01EtherType0x0800 (IPv4)FCSCRCTCP/IP is stateful, but everything below is stateless. None of the lower layers know or care about the payload, so nothing prevents us from sending new IP packets or even Ethernet frames with our own handcrafted binary data. Whatâs the absolute minimum state we need to preserve for each guest? We can ignore server initialisation and assume switches only happen within the main loop. Letâs take another look at the loop.for (int client; (client = accept(server, NULL, NULL)) >= 0;) { if (recv(client, NULL, 4096, MSG_TRUNC) > 0) send(client, reply, sizeof reply - 1, MSG_NOSIGNAL); close(client); }This code has only two state variables, server and client. Both are simple file descriptors. If our scheduler had to suspend a guest at any syscall point in this loop, weâd need to save the following state:struct Context { pc: u64, // Instruction address to return to fd_server: i32, // server file descriptor fd_client: i32, // client file descriptor }We only need 16 bytes per guest, so to run 15M guests in our runtime and be able to suspend them and switch between them, we would need 220MB of RAM. Weâre playing it safe here - we donât need the full u64 or i32, and with bit packing, we could probably get down to 8 bytes per guest. What else do we need?$ codex "How much TCP connection takes?" Idle connection takes zero, while active connection consumes around 5kbHereâs what we get if we assume that only 1M of connections are active at any given moment.14M idle connections - 0 bytesScheduling state for 15M guests - 220MB1M active connections at 5kb each = 5GBOnly half of our RAM budget, yay! I donât often exceed my own expectations. Letâs validate it:int main(void) { // Run 14M of idle connections - just listen and do nothing // SHOULD CONSUMER ZERO MEMORY! for (int i = 0; i < 14_000_000; i++) { int server = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0); listen(server, 1) } }$ ./run_15m_idle_servers Out Of Memory $ dmesg | tail -n 1 'SHOULD CONSUMER ZERO MEMORY' - what are you, stupid?Looks like the kernel still has some leftover comments from Linus. Iâm not stupid, but I have to admit I was below average here. While an LLM is a significant boost for learning, itâs too easy to accept its answers without thinking critically and feel ashamed later on. But hey, embarrassment is a cost of entry - letâs dive into networking in the kernel.Networking in the kernelRemember when I said that our simple server uses only 5 syscalls? I missed one that was right in front of me. Let me highlight it:int main(void) { for (int i = 0; i < 10_000_000; ++i) { socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0); } }That code will OOM in a 10GB VM. The socket syscall allocates around 3300 bytes, whether the socket is active, listening, or doing nothing. Linux was clearly written at a time when RAM was big and cheap, sigh. Letâs ask AI where all the memory goes:StructsizeofDescriptionstruct tcp_sock2240BTCP state, queues, timers, sequence numbers, congestion control, retransmit and delivery bookkeepingstruct socket_alloc768BA 128-byte struct socket with an embedded wait queue, plus a 608-byte sockfs inode and paddingstruct file184BOpen-file operations, flags, credentials, reference count, and the pointer back to the socketstruct dentry192BThe sockfs pseudo-name, inode pointer, and VFS lookup and lifetime bookkeepingTotal3384BPotato-potato, TLDR, the kernel uses that memory for many things. I was hoping to see some buffer: [u8; 3300], but it doesnât look like we can optimise much without rewriting half the kernel. Itâs Sunday and I have few tokens left, so weâll have to think instead - how can we bypass the kernel?What are our options? Engineering wisdom tells us that if we canât solve a task, weâre working at the wrong level of abstraction. Hmâ¦ level, level, layer. Wait a minute! We started with HTTP and went level down to TCP/IP. That reminds me of something! Mom! Mom! Where are all my university books? I NEED THEM NOW!The OSI model isâ¦ umâ¦ something I studied at university. Yeah, thatâs a good definition. We started with our app and HTTP at the application layer, then went down to TCP/IP at the transport layer. There are three more layers below that. Can we go lower to get full control and solve our RAM problem? Oh yes, thatâs exactly what operating at the network and data link layers lets us do. What about going to the lowest?Clear.Each layer wraps the data from the layer above it.HTTPApplication Â· L7TCPTransport Â· L4IPNetwork Â· L3EthernetData link Â· L2PhysicalL1GET /PayloadPayloadPayloadâ¡ð©Src port50123Dst port80Seq1001ACK7001FlagsACKSrc IP192.0.2.10Dst IP198.51.100.20TTL64Protocol6 (TCP)Dst MAC02:00:00:00:00:feSrc MAC02:00:00:00:00:01EtherType0x0800 (IPv4)FCSCRCTCP/IP is stateful, but everything below is stateless. None of the lower layers know or care about the payload, so nothing prevents us from sending new IP packets or even Ethernet frames with our own handcrafted binary data. We just need to reimplement the TCP and IP stack ourselves. Are there any examples? Embedded folks have solved this millions of times. The most extreme case is Adam Dunkelsâs Miniweb - it combines a custom TCP/IP implementation and an HTTP server that together need only 28 bytes (B-Y-T-E-S) to work.There are plenty of less extreme, more mature implementations, though:gVisor essentially reimplements the whole OS, including the network stackSeastar is a whole runtime with its own network stack from the amazing team behind ScyllaDBlwIP is a popular C implementation of TCP/IPF-Stack uses FreeBSDâs TCP/IP stackGo, C++, and C are nice languages (well, kinda), but theyâre nowhere near as nice as our beloved Rust. So weâll take smoltcp, which is purely amazing:>smoltcp is a standalone, event-driven TCP/IP stack that is designed for >bare-metal, real-time systems. smoltcp does not need heap allocation at allSo, cargo add smoltcp and call it a day? Not so fast. smoltcp is essentially a smart parser and canât do I/O. How does it communicate with your network card? There are 3 ways:TUN is a virtual network interface that the kernel provides. It operates at the IP layerTAP is another kernel feature that operates one layer down, at the Ethernet layerDPDK lets you communicate directly with the network card using Ethernet frames, completely bypassing the kernelsmoltcp is kind enough to provide the first two options out of the box, and theyâre easy to use.fn main() -> std::io::Result<()> { let (mut device, config) = match std::env::args().nth(1).as_deref() { Some("tun") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltun", Medium::Ip)?, Config::new(HardwareAddress::Ip), ), Some("tap") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltap", Medium::Ethernet)?, Config::new(EthernetAddress([0x02, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2]).into()), ), _ => panic!("pass tun or tap"), }; let mut iface = Interface::new(config, &mut device, Instant::now()); iface.update_ip_addrs(|addrs| { addrs.push(IpCidr::new(IpAddress::v4(192, 0, 2, 2), 24)).unwrap(); }); let mut sockets = SocketSet::new(Vec::new()); // Main network loop loop { let now = Instant::now(); iface.poll(now, &mut device, &mut sockets); wait(device.as_raw_fd(), iface.poll_delay(now, &sockets))?; } }But what about the third option, DPDK? When you care about latency A LOT, every syscall is a bloody wound. The syscall itself might take 500ns, and copying data from the kernel to userspace might take another 1000ns, depending on the buffer size. That sounds tiny, but for perspective: itâs common for high-frequency trading firms to saturate 400Gbs network interfaces. That means processing up to 600 million packets per second, or roughly 1.7ns per packet.DPDK lets your program communicate directly with the hardware network card using Ethernet frames, bypassing the entire kernel network stack. You read from and write to a memory-mapped buffer without syscalls: when you want to send data - you simply write it to the buffer and youâre done; when you want to read what youâve received - itâs already there.Thereâs another, less obvious advantage - what happens when a new packet arrives at your network device? An interrupt fires, the CPU core stops executing your app, switches to the kernel, processes the interrupt, and then goes back to your app. Now imagine A LOT of incoming packets, so many that your app gets interrupted all the time. It has a beautiful name, livelock, and I guess debugging it in production is its own level of hell. DPDK lets you avoid all that, but thereâs a cost.DPDK works by running a busy loop that takes up an entire CPU core. It constantly moves data between memory and the network device in both directions, kinda like that:loop { send_from_app_memory_to_network(); receive_from_network_to_app_memory(); }Thereâs another, less obvious nuance. DPDK requires exclusive access to the network device because it uses a driver that operates at the hardware level - two drivers canât work with the same device at the same time. Once you enable the DPDK driver for your network interfaceâ¦ it disappears from the OS. Yep, nothing in ifconfig, if you enable it over SSH, youâll lose SSH access too. I can imagine a lot of funny stories coming out of that.Actually, thereâs a fourth option now - Linux now has an AF_XDP subsystem that does something similar. It lets you bypass the kernel network stack and uses the same idea of memory-mapped buffers to get zero-copy I/O in your app. The kernel still owns the device and interrupts still happen as usual. When a new packet arrives, the kernel runs a simple eBPF filter that decides whether to send it directly to the userspace buffer, bypassing the network stack, or process it normally. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought. The syscall itself might take 500ns, and copying data from the kernel to userspace might take another 1000ns, depending on the buffer size. That sounds tiny, but for perspective: itâs common for high-frequency trading firms to saturate 400Gbs network interfaces. That means processing up to 600 million packets per second, or roughly 1.7ns per packet.DPDK lets your program communicate directly with the hardware network card using Ethernet frames, bypassing the entire kernel network stack. You read from and write to a memory-mapped buffer without syscalls: when you want to send data - you simply write it to the buffer and youâre done; when you want to read what youâve received - itâs already there.Thereâs another, less obvious advantage - what happens when a new packet arrives at your network device? An interrupt fires, the CPU core stops executing your app, switches to the kernel, processes the interrupt, and then goes back to your app. Now imagine A LOT of incoming packets, so many that your app gets interrupted all the time. It has a beautiful name, livelock, and I guess debugging it in production is its own level of hell. DPDK lets you avoid all that, but thereâs a cost.DPDK works by running a busy loop that takes up an entire CPU core. It constantly moves data between memory and the network device in both directions, kinda like that:loop { send_from_app_memory_to_network(); receive_from_network_to_app_memory(); }Thereâs another, less obvious nuance. DPDK requires exclusive access to the network device because it uses a driver that operates at the hardware level - two drivers canât work with the same device at the same time. Once you enable the DPDK driver for your network interfaceâ¦ it disappears from the OS. Yep, nothing in ifconfig, if you enable it over SSH, youâll lose SSH access too. I can imagine a lot of funny stories coming out of that.Actually, thereâs a fourth option now - Linux now has an AF_XDP subsystem that does something similar. It lets you bypass the kernel network stack and uses the same idea of memory-mapped buffers to get zero-copy I/O in your app. The kernel still owns the device and interrupts still happen as usual. When a new packet arrives, the kernel runs a simple eBPF filter that decides whether to send it directly to the userspace buffer, bypassing the network stack, or process it normally. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought. And another article.Referenceshttps://github.com/artemyarulin/AI10M/tree/main/poc/ten-million-webservers sourcehttps://medium.com/beyond-localhost/how-we-handle-15M-websocket-connections-on-a-single-server-8917c3952f59 great overview of DPDKhttps://doc.dpdk.org/guides/index.html DPDK main documentation sitehttps://docs.kernel.org/networking/af_xdp.html AF_XDP overview

Hereâs what we get if we assume that only 1M of connections are active at any given moment.14M idle connections - 0 bytesScheduling state for 15M guests - 220MB1M active connections at 5kb each = 5GBOnly half of our RAM budget, yay! I donât often exceed my own expectations. Letâs validate it:int main(void) { // Run 14M of idle connections - just listen and do nothing // SHOULD CONSUMER ZERO MEMORY! for (int i = 0; i < 14_000_000; i++) { int server = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0); listen(server, 1) } }$ ./run_15m_idle_servers Out Of Memory $ dmesg | tail -n 1 'SHOULD CONSUMER ZERO MEMORY' - what are you, stupid?Looks like the kernel still has some leftover comments from Linus. Iâm not stupid, but I have to admit I was below average here. While an LLM is a significant boost for learning, itâs too easy to accept its answers without thinking critically and feel ashamed later on. But hey, embarrassment is a cost of entry - letâs dive into networking in the kernel.Networking in the kernelRemember when I said that our simple server uses only 5 syscalls? I missed one that was right in front of me. Let me highlight it:int main(void) { for (int i = 0; i < 10_000_000; ++i) { socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0); } }That code will OOM in a 10GB VM. The socket syscall allocates around 3300 bytes, whether the socket is active, listening, or doing nothing. Linux was clearly written at a time when RAM was big and cheap, sigh. Letâs ask AI where all the memory goes:StructsizeofDescriptionstruct tcp_sock2240BTCP state, queues, timers, sequence numbers, congestion control, retransmit and delivery bookkeepingstruct socket_alloc768BA 128-byte struct socket with an embedded wait queue, plus a 608-byte sockfs inode and paddingstruct file184BOpen-file operations, flags, credentials, reference count, and the pointer back to the socketstruct dentry192BThe sockfs pseudo-name, inode pointer, and VFS lookup and lifetime bookkeepingTotal3384BPotato-potato, TLDR, the kernel uses that memory for many things. I was hoping to see some buffer: [u8; 3300], but it doesnât look like we can optimise much without rewriting half the kernel. Itâs Sunday and I have few tokens left, so weâll have to think instead - how can we bypass the kernel?What are our options? Engineering wisdom tells us that if we canât solve a task, weâre working at the wrong level of abstraction. Hmâ¦ level, level, layer. Wait a minute! We started with HTTP and went level down to TCP/IP. That reminds me of something! Mom! Mom! Where are all my university books? I NEED THEM NOW!The OSI model isâ¦ umâ¦ something I studied at university. Yeah, thatâs a good definition. We started with our app and HTTP at the application layer, then went down to TCP/IP at the transport layer. There are three more layers below that. Can we go lower to get full control and solve our RAM problem? Oh yes, thatâs exactly what operating at the network and data link layers lets us do. What about going to the lowest?Clear.Each layer wraps the data from the layer above it.HTTPApplication Â· L7TCPTransport Â· L4IPNetwork Â· L3EthernetData link Â· L2PhysicalL1GET /PayloadPayloadPayloadâ¡ð©Src port50123Dst port80Seq1001ACK7001FlagsACKSrc IP192.0.2.10Dst IP198.51.100.20TTL64Protocol6 (TCP)Dst MAC02:00:00:00:00:feSrc MAC02:00:00:00:00:01EtherType0x0800 (IPv4)FCSCRCTCP/IP is stateful, but everything below is stateless. None of the lower layers know or care about the payload, so nothing prevents us from sending new IP packets or even Ethernet frames with our own handcrafted binary data. We just need to reimplement the TCP and IP stack ourselves. Are there any examples? Embedded folks have solved this millions of times. The most extreme case is Adam Dunkelsâs Miniweb - it combines a custom TCP/IP implementation and an HTTP server that together need only 28 bytes (B-Y-T-E-S) to work.There are plenty of less extreme, more mature implementations, though:gVisor essentially reimplements the whole OS, including the network stackSeastar is a whole runtime with its own network stack from the amazing team behind ScyllaDBlwIP is a popular C implementation of TCP/IPF-Stack uses FreeBSDâs TCP/IP stackGo, C++, and C are nice languages (well, kinda), but theyâre nowhere near as nice as our beloved Rust. So weâll take smoltcp, which is purely amazing:>smoltcp is a standalone, event-driven TCP/IP stack that is designed for >bare-metal, real-time systems. smoltcp does not need heap allocation at allSo, cargo add smoltcp and call it a day? So weâll take smoltcp, which is purely amazing:>smoltcp is a standalone, event-driven TCP/IP stack that is designed for >bare-metal, real-time systems. smoltcp does not need heap allocation at allSo, cargo add smoltcp and call it a day? Not so fast. smoltcp is essentially a smart parser and canât do I/O. How does it communicate with your network card? There are 3 ways:TUN is a virtual network interface that the kernel provides. It operates at the IP layerTAP is another kernel feature that operates one layer down, at the Ethernet layerDPDK lets you communicate directly with the network card using Ethernet frames, completely bypassing the kernelsmoltcp is kind enough to provide the first two options out of the box, and theyâre easy to use.fn main() -> std::io::Result<()> { let (mut device, config) = match std::env::args().nth(1).as_deref() { Some("tun") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltun", Medium::Ip)?, Config::new(HardwareAddress::Ip), ), Some("tap") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltap", Medium::Ethernet)?, Config::new(EthernetAddress([0x02, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2]).into()), ), _ => panic!("pass tun or tap"), }; let mut iface = Interface::new(config, &mut device, Instant::now()); iface.update_ip_addrs(|addrs| { addrs.push(IpCidr::new(IpAddress::v4(192, 0, 2, 2), 24)).unwrap(); }); let mut sockets = SocketSet::new(Vec::new()); // Main network loop loop { let now = Instant::now(); iface.poll(now, &mut device, &mut sockets); wait(device.as_raw_fd(), iface.poll_delay(now, &sockets))?; } }But what about the third option, DPDK? When you care about latency A LOT, every syscall is a bloody wound. The syscall itself might take 500ns, and copying data from the kernel to userspace might take another 1000ns, depending on the buffer size. That sounds tiny, but for perspective: itâs common for high-frequency trading firms to saturate 400Gbs network interfaces. That means processing up to 600 million packets per second, or roughly 1.7ns per packet.DPDK lets your program communicate directly with the hardware network card using Ethernet frames, bypassing the entire kernel network stack. You read from and write to a memory-mapped buffer without syscalls: when you want to send data - you simply write it to the buffer and youâre done; when you want to read what youâve received - itâs already there.Thereâs another, less obvious advantage - what happens when a new packet arrives at your network device? An interrupt fires, the CPU core stops executing your app, switches to the kernel, processes the interrupt, and then goes back to your app. Now imagine A LOT of incoming packets, so many that your app gets interrupted all the time. It has a beautiful name, livelock, and I guess debugging it in production is its own level of hell. DPDK lets you avoid all that, but thereâs a cost.DPDK works by running a busy loop that takes up an entire CPU core. It constantly moves data between memory and the network device in both directions, kinda like that:loop { send_from_app_memory_to_network(); receive_from_network_to_app_memory(); }Thereâs another, less obvious nuance. DPDK requires exclusive access to the network device because it uses a driver that operates at the hardware level - two drivers canât work with the same device at the same time. Once you enable the DPDK driver for your network interfaceâ¦ it disappears from the OS. Yep, nothing in ifconfig, if you enable it over SSH, youâll lose SSH access too. I can imagine a lot of funny stories coming out of that.Actually, thereâs a fourth option now - Linux now has an AF_XDP subsystem that does something similar. It lets you bypass the kernel network stack and uses the same idea of memory-mapped buffers to get zero-copy I/O in your app. The kernel still owns the device and interrupts still happen as usual. When a new packet arrives, the kernel runs a simple eBPF filter that decides whether to send it directly to the userspace buffer, bypassing the network stack, or process it normally. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought. And another article.Referenceshttps://github.com/artemyarulin/AI10M/tree/main/poc/ten-million-webservers sourcehttps://medium.com/beyond-localhost/how-we-handle-15M-websocket-connections-on-a-single-server-8917c3952f59 great overview of DPDKhttps://doc.dpdk.org/guides/index.html DPDK main documentation sitehttps://docs.kernel.org/networking/af_xdp.html AF_XDP overview

Remember when I said that our simple server uses only 5 syscalls? I missed one that was right in front of me. Let me highlight it:int main(void) { for (int i = 0; i < 10_000_000; ++i) { socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0); } }That code will OOM in a 10GB VM. The socket syscall allocates around 3300 bytes, whether the socket is active, listening, or doing nothing. Linux was clearly written at a time when RAM was big and cheap, sigh. Letâs ask AI where all the memory goes:StructsizeofDescriptionstruct tcp_sock2240BTCP state, queues, timers, sequence numbers, congestion control, retransmit and delivery bookkeepingstruct socket_alloc768BA 128-byte struct socket with an embedded wait queue, plus a 608-byte sockfs inode and paddingstruct file184BOpen-file operations, flags, credentials, reference count, and the pointer back to the socketstruct dentry192BThe sockfs pseudo-name, inode pointer, and VFS lookup and lifetime bookkeepingTotal3384BPotato-potato, TLDR, the kernel uses that memory for many things. I was hoping to see some buffer: [u8; 3300], but it doesnât look like we can optimise much without rewriting half the kernel. Itâs Sunday and I have few tokens left, so weâll have to think instead - how can we bypass the kernel?What are our options? Engineering wisdom tells us that if we canât solve a task, weâre working at the wrong level of abstraction. Hmâ¦ level, level, layer. Wait a minute! We started with HTTP and went level down to TCP/IP. That reminds me of something! Mom! Mom! Where are all my university books? I NEED THEM NOW!The OSI model isâ¦ umâ¦ something I studied at university. Yeah, thatâs a good definition. We started with our app and HTTP at the application layer, then went down to TCP/IP at the transport layer. There are three more layers below that. Can we go lower to get full control and solve our RAM problem? Oh yes, thatâs exactly what operating at the network and data link layers lets us do. What about going to the lowest?Clear.Each layer wraps the data from the layer above it.HTTPApplication Â· L7TCPTransport Â· L4IPNetwork Â· L3EthernetData link Â· L2PhysicalL1GET /PayloadPayloadPayloadâ¡ð©Src port50123Dst port80Seq1001ACK7001FlagsACKSrc IP192.0.2.10Dst IP198.51.100.20TTL64Protocol6 (TCP)Dst MAC02:00:00:00:00:feSrc MAC02:00:00:00:00:01EtherType0x0800 (IPv4)FCSCRCTCP/IP is stateful, but everything below is stateless. An interrupt fires, the CPU core stops executing your app, switches to the kernel, processes the interrupt, and then goes back to your app. Now imagine A LOT of incoming packets, so many that your app gets interrupted all the time. It has a beautiful name, livelock, and I guess debugging it in production is its own level of hell. DPDK lets you avoid all that, but thereâs a cost.DPDK works by running a busy loop that takes up an entire CPU core. It constantly moves data between memory and the network device in both directions, kinda like that:loop { send_from_app_memory_to_network(); receive_from_network_to_app_memory(); }Thereâs another, less obvious nuance. DPDK requires exclusive access to the network device because it uses a driver that operates at the hardware level - two drivers canât work with the same device at the same time. Once you enable the DPDK driver for your network interfaceâ¦ it disappears from the OS. Yep, nothing in ifconfig, if you enable it over SSH, youâll lose SSH access too. I can imagine a lot of funny stories coming out of that.Actually, thereâs a fourth option now - Linux now has an AF_XDP subsystem that does something similar. It lets you bypass the kernel network stack and uses the same idea of memory-mapped buffers to get zero-copy I/O in your app. The kernel still owns the device and interrupts still happen as usual. When a new packet arrives, the kernel runs a simple eBPF filter that decides whether to send it directly to the userspace buffer, bypassing the network stack, or process it normally. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought. And another article.Referenceshttps://github.com/artemyarulin/AI10M/tree/main/poc/ten-million-webservers sourcehttps://medium.com/beyond-localhost/how-we-handle-15M-websocket-connections-on-a-single-server-8917c3952f59 great overview of DPDKhttps://doc.dpdk.org/guides/index.html DPDK main documentation sitehttps://docs.kernel.org/networking/af_xdp.html AF_XDP overview

What are our options? Engineering wisdom tells us that if we canât solve a task, weâre working at the wrong level of abstraction. Hmâ¦ level, level, layer. Wait a minute! We started with HTTP and went level down to TCP/IP. That reminds me of something! Mom! Mom! Where are all my university books? I NEED THEM NOW!The OSI model isâ¦ umâ¦ something I studied at university. Yeah, thatâs a good definition. We started with our app and HTTP at the application layer, then went down to TCP/IP at the transport layer. There are three more layers below that. Can we go lower to get full control and solve our RAM problem? Oh yes, thatâs exactly what operating at the network and data link layers lets us do. What about going to the lowest?Clear.Each layer wraps the data from the layer above it.HTTPApplication Â· L7TCPTransport Â· L4IPNetwork Â· L3EthernetData link Â· L2PhysicalL1GET /PayloadPayloadPayloadâ¡ð©Src port50123Dst port80Seq1001ACK7001FlagsACKSrc IP192.0.2.10Dst IP198.51.100.20TTL64Protocol6 (TCP)Dst MAC02:00:00:00:00:feSrc MAC02:00:00:00:00:01EtherType0x0800 (IPv4)FCSCRCTCP/IP is stateful, but everything below is stateless. None of the lower layers know or care about the payload, so nothing prevents us from sending new IP packets or even Ethernet frames with our own handcrafted binary data. We just need to reimplement the TCP and IP stack ourselves. Are there any examples? Embedded folks have solved this millions of times. The most extreme case is Adam Dunkelsâs Miniweb - it combines a custom TCP/IP implementation and an HTTP server that together need only 28 bytes (B-Y-T-E-S) to work.There are plenty of less extreme, more mature implementations, though:gVisor essentially reimplements the whole OS, including the network stackSeastar is a whole runtime with its own network stack from the amazing team behind ScyllaDBlwIP is a popular C implementation of TCP/IPF-Stack uses FreeBSDâs TCP/IP stackGo, C++, and C are nice languages (well, kinda), but theyâre nowhere near as nice as our beloved Rust. So weâll take smoltcp, which is purely amazing:>smoltcp is a standalone, event-driven TCP/IP stack that is designed for >bare-metal, real-time systems. smoltcp does not need heap allocation at allSo, cargo add smoltcp and call it a day? Not so fast. smoltcp is essentially a smart parser and canât do I/O. How does it communicate with your network card? There are 3 ways:TUN is a virtual network interface that the kernel provides. It operates at the IP layerTAP is another kernel feature that operates one layer down, at the Ethernet layerDPDK lets you communicate directly with the network card using Ethernet frames, completely bypassing the kernelsmoltcp is kind enough to provide the first two options out of the box, and theyâre easy to use.fn main() -> std::io::Result<()> { let (mut device, config) = match std::env::args().nth(1).as_deref() { Some("tun") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltun", Medium::Ip)?, Config::new(HardwareAddress::Ip), ), Some("tap") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltap", Medium::Ethernet)?, Config::new(EthernetAddress([0x02, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2]).into()), ), _ => panic!("pass tun or tap"), }; let mut iface = Interface::new(config, &mut device, Instant::now()); iface.update_ip_addrs(|addrs| { addrs.push(IpCidr::new(IpAddress::v4(192, 0, 2, 2), 24)).unwrap(); }); let mut sockets = SocketSet::new(Vec::new()); // Main network loop loop { let now = Instant::now(); iface.poll(now, &mut device, &mut sockets); wait(device.as_raw_fd(), iface.poll_delay(now, &sockets))?; } }But what about the third option, DPDK? When you care about latency A LOT, every syscall is a bloody wound. The syscall itself might take 500ns, and copying data from the kernel to userspace might take another 1000ns, depending on the buffer size. That sounds tiny, but for perspective: itâs common for high-frequency trading firms to saturate 400Gbs network interfaces. That means processing up to 600 million packets per second, or roughly 1.7ns per packet.DPDK lets your program communicate directly with the hardware network card using Ethernet frames, bypassing the entire kernel network stack. It operates at the IP layerTAP is another kernel feature that operates one layer down, at the Ethernet layerDPDK lets you communicate directly with the network card using Ethernet frames, completely bypassing the kernelsmoltcp is kind enough to provide the first two options out of the box, and theyâre easy to use.fn main() -> std::io::Result<()> { let (mut device, config) = match std::env::args().nth(1).as_deref() { Some("tun") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltun", Medium::Ip)?, Config::new(HardwareAddress::Ip), ), Some("tap") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltap", Medium::Ethernet)?, Config::new(EthernetAddress([0x02, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2]).into()), ), _ => panic!("pass tun or tap"), }; let mut iface = Interface::new(config, &mut device, Instant::now()); iface.update_ip_addrs(|addrs| { addrs.push(IpCidr::new(IpAddress::v4(192, 0, 2, 2), 24)).unwrap(); }); let mut sockets = SocketSet::new(Vec::new()); // Main network loop loop { let now = Instant::now(); iface.poll(now, &mut device, &mut sockets); wait(device.as_raw_fd(), iface.poll_delay(now, &sockets))?; } }But what about the third option, DPDK? When you care about latency A LOT, every syscall is a bloody wound. The syscall itself might take 500ns, and copying data from the kernel to userspace might take another 1000ns, depending on the buffer size. That sounds tiny, but for perspective: itâs common for high-frequency trading firms to saturate 400Gbs network interfaces. That means processing up to 600 million packets per second, or roughly 1.7ns per packet.DPDK lets your program communicate directly with the hardware network card using Ethernet frames, bypassing the entire kernel network stack. You read from and write to a memory-mapped buffer without syscalls: when you want to send data - you simply write it to the buffer and youâre done; when you want to read what youâve received - itâs already there.Thereâs another, less obvious advantage - what happens when a new packet arrives at your network device? An interrupt fires, the CPU core stops executing your app, switches to the kernel, processes the interrupt, and then goes back to your app. Now imagine A LOT of incoming packets, so many that your app gets interrupted all the time. It has a beautiful name, livelock, and I guess debugging it in production is its own level of hell. DPDK lets you avoid all that, but thereâs a cost.DPDK works by running a busy loop that takes up an entire CPU core. It constantly moves data between memory and the network device in both directions, kinda like that:loop { send_from_app_memory_to_network(); receive_from_network_to_app_memory(); }Thereâs another, less obvious nuance. DPDK requires exclusive access to the network device because it uses a driver that operates at the hardware level - two drivers canât work with the same device at the same time. Once you enable the DPDK driver for your network interfaceâ¦ it disappears from the OS. Yep, nothing in ifconfig, if you enable it over SSH, youâll lose SSH access too. I can imagine a lot of funny stories coming out of that.Actually, thereâs a fourth option now - Linux now has an AF_XDP subsystem that does something similar. It lets you bypass the kernel network stack and uses the same idea of memory-mapped buffers to get zero-copy I/O in your app. The kernel still owns the device and interrupts still happen as usual. When a new packet arrives, the kernel runs a simple eBPF filter that decides whether to send it directly to the userspace buffer, bypassing the network stack, or process it normally. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought. And another article.Referenceshttps://github.com/artemyarulin/AI10M/tree/main/poc/ten-million-webservers sourcehttps://medium.com/beyond-localhost/how-we-handle-15M-websocket-connections-on-a-single-server-8917c3952f59 great overview of DPDKhttps://doc.dpdk.org/guides/index.html DPDK main documentation sitehttps://docs.kernel.org/networking/af_xdp.html AF_XDP overview
