Once you enable the DPDK driver for your network interfaceâ¦ it disappears from the OS. Yep, nothing in ifconfig, if you enable it over SSH, youâll lose SSH access too. I can imagine a lot of funny stories coming out of that.Actually, thereâs a fourth option now - Linux now has an AF_XDP subsystem that does something similar. It lets you bypass the kernel network stack and uses the same idea of memory-mapped buffers to get zero-copy I/O in your app. The kernel still owns the device and interrupts still happen as usual. When a new packet arrives, the kernel runs a simple eBPF filter that decides whether to send it directly to the userspace buffer, bypassing the network stack, or process it normally. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought. And another article.Referenceshttps://github.com/artemyarulin/AI10M/tree/main/poc/ten-million-webservers sourcehttps://medium.com/beyond-localhost/how-we-handle-15M-websocket-connections-on-a-single-server-8917c3952f59 great overview of DPDKhttps://doc.dpdk.org/guides/index.html DPDK main documentation sitehttps://docs.kernel.org/networking/af_xdp.html AF_XDP overview

So, cargo add smoltcp and call it a day? Not so fast. smoltcp is essentially a smart parser and canât do I/O. How does it communicate with your network card? There are 3 ways:TUN is a virtual network interface that the kernel provides. It operates at the IP layerTAP is another kernel feature that operates one layer down, at the Ethernet layerDPDK lets you communicate directly with the network card using Ethernet frames, completely bypassing the kernelsmoltcp is kind enough to provide the first two options out of the box, and theyâre easy to use.fn main() -> std::io::Result<()> { let (mut device, config) = match std::env::args().nth(1).as_deref() { Some("tun") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltun", Medium::Ip)?, Config::new(HardwareAddress::Ip), ), Some("tap") => ( TunTapInterface::new("smoltap", Medium::Ethernet)?, Config::new(EthernetAddress([0x02, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2]).into()), ), _ => panic!("pass tun or tap"), }; let mut iface = Interface::new(config, &mut device, Instant::now()); iface.update_ip_addrs(|addrs| { addrs.push(IpCidr::new(IpAddress::v4(192, 0, 2, 2), 24)).unwrap(); }); let mut sockets = SocketSet::new(Vec::new()); // Main network loop loop { let now = Instant::now(); iface.poll(now, &mut device, &mut sockets); wait(device.as_raw_fd(), iface.poll_delay(now, &sockets))?; } }But what about the third option, DPDK? When you care about latency A LOT, every syscall is a bloody wound. The syscall itself might take 500ns, and copying data from the kernel to userspace might take another 1000ns, depending on the buffer size. That sounds tiny, but for perspective: itâs common for high-frequency trading firms to saturate 400Gbs network interfaces. That means processing up to 600 million packets per second, or roughly 1.7ns per packet.DPDK lets your program communicate directly with the hardware network card using Ethernet frames, bypassing the entire kernel network stack. You read from and write to a memory-mapped buffer without syscalls: when you want to send data - you simply write it to the buffer and youâre done; when you want to read what youâve received - itâs already there.Thereâs another, less obvious advantage - what happens when a new packet arrives at your network device? An interrupt fires, the CPU core stops executing your app, switches to the kernel, processes the interrupt, and then goes back to your app. Now imagine A LOT of incoming packets, so many that your app gets interrupted all the time. It has a beautiful name, livelock, and I guess debugging it in production is its own level of hell. DPDK lets you avoid all that, but thereâs a cost.DPDK works by running a busy loop that takes up an entire CPU core. It constantly moves data between memory and the network device in both directions, kinda like that:loop { send_from_app_memory_to_network(); receive_from_network_to_app_memory(); }Thereâs another, less obvious nuance. DPDK requires exclusive access to the network device because it uses a driver that operates at the hardware level - two drivers canât work with the same device at the same time. Once you enable the DPDK driver for your network interfaceâ¦ it disappears from the OS. Yep, nothing in ifconfig, if you enable it over SSH, youâll lose SSH access too. I can imagine a lot of funny stories coming out of that.Actually, thereâs a fourth option now - Linux now has an AF_XDP subsystem that does something similar. It lets you bypass the kernel network stack and uses the same idea of memory-mapped buffers to get zero-copy I/O in your app. The kernel still owns the device and interrupts still happen as usual. When a new packet arrives, the kernel runs a simple eBPF filter that decides whether to send it directly to the userspace buffer, bypassing the network stack, or process it normally. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought. That means SSH still works. AF_XDP supports an alternative mode, SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL, which is even closer to DPDK: you get a busy loop with no interrupts at the cost of a CPU core. DPDK still beats it because it bypasses the kernel completely, but AF_XDP is very close (and simply easier to manage).To support DPDK and AF_XDP in smoltcp, we only need to implement the Device trait with 3 methods if we want full control. Alternatively, we can use the existing crates rust-dpdk-net for DPDK and xsk-rs for AF_XDP to help us.Now the final flow is:The runtime loads 15M web server guests into its memoryThe runtime scheduler starts executing guests one by oneGuest syscalls are intercepted by the runtime and replaced by the smoltcp network stack and its adaptersAll 15M guests start listening, each on its own addressThe scheduler polls for incoming data from smoltcp adapters, wakes up a guest, makes it process the data, and switches to the nextAnd now we can finally finish the article. The kernel network stack uses up all the RAM at around 2.5M web servers, and RPS drops as we run more servers. With smoltcp, RAM usage grows slower - 620 bytes per idle server and around a kilobyte for an active one, we got to 16M of servers. DPDK is clearly the beast in this benchmark, with AF_XDP just behind it:Why is this so exciting? It means that by moving the network stack to userland, you can get full control on RAM usage and improve performance. And with custom runtime, you can simply run guests faster.Simply is a bit of a stretch - building a proof of concept is easy. Building a compatible Linux runtime and reimplementing all the syscalls with 100% POSIX compatibility take a bit more thought.
